    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\n\\paragraph{Datasets.}\nWe evaluate on three English IE benchmarks spanning a difficulty gradient.\n\\textbf{SciERC} \\cite{luan2018scierc} contains 551 test instances annotated for joint NER (6 types) and RE (7 types).\n\\textbf{CoNLL-2003} \\cite{tjongkimsang2003conll} provides 3{,}453 test sentences with 4 entity types, where models achieve ${>}$91\\% micro F1.\n\\textbf{WNUT-17} \\cite{derczynski2017wnut} consists of 1{,}287 social media instances with emerging entities, used to probe method boundaries (54\\% zero F1).\n\n\\paragraph{Models and sampling.}\nBoth \\textbf{Qwen3-8B} and \\textbf{LLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct} are fine-tuned with LoRA (rank$=$32, $\\alpha{=}64$) for 5 epochs using LLaMA Factory under identical training procedures, ensuring cross-model comparisons reflect architectural differences rather than training confounds.\nWe draw $N{=}8$ samples at $T{=}1.0$ (primary), with $N{=}16$ for scaling experiments.\\footnote{$N{=}16$ results for CoNLL (Qwen) and SciERC (LLaMA) are in Table~\\ref{tab:robustness} (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}).}\nQuality estimation uses Spearman $\\rho$ and AUROC; downstream utility uses best-of-$N$ selection F1 with exact span matching (NER) and head-tail-label matching (RE).\nInference uses vLLM with constrained decoding \\cite{willard2023guided}.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\subsection{Quality Estimation Results}\n\\label{sec:qe_results}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Quality estimation across tasks and models (Spearman $\\rho$ and AUROC). Best value per block and column in \\textbf{bold}. SciERC evaluates joint NER (6 types) and RE (7 types); CoNLL evaluates NER (4 types). $N{=}8$ results use seed${=}42$; $N{=}16$ results are averaged across seeds \\{42, 123, 456\\}.}\n\\label{tab:quality_estimation}\n\\begin{tabular}{ll cc cc cc cc}\n\\toprule\n& & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{SciERC NER}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{SciERC RE}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{CoNLL (Qwen)}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{CoNLL (LLaMA)}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){3-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-6} \\cmidrule(lr){7-8} \\cmidrule(lr){9-10}\nSignal & Family & $\\rho$ & AUC & $\\rho$ & AUC & $\\rho$ & AUC & $\\rho$ & AUC \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{10}{l}{\\textit{$N{=}8$}} \\\\\nVoting Conf. & Structural & \\textbf{.379} & .684 & \\textbf{.350} & .693 & .424 & .789 & .426 & .709 \\\\\nSoft Jaccard  & Structural & .360 & .692 & .250 & .670 & .436 & .801 & \\textbf{.431} & .704 \\\\\nFleiss' $\\kappa$ & Surface & .267 & .629 & .275 & .609 & .400 & .774 & .428 & .703 \\\\\nExact Match  & Surface    & .295 & \\textbf{.749} & .134 & \\textbf{.706} & \\textbf{.451} & \\textbf{.816} & \\textbf{.431} & .705 \\\\\nLog-prob     & Model      & .205 & .578 & .266 & .600 & .316 & .753 & .307 & \\textbf{.761} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{10}{l}{\\textit{$N{=}16$ (3-seed)}} \\\\\nVoting Conf. & Structural & & & & & & & .458${\\pm}$.003 & \\\\\nSoft Jaccard  & Structural & & & & & & & \\textbf{.463${\\pm}$.007} & \\\\\nFleiss' $\\kappa$ & Surface & & & & & & & .455${\\pm}$.006 & \\\\\nExact Match  & Surface    & & & & & & & .460${\\pm}$.008 & \\\\\nLog-prob     & Model      & & & & & & & .310${\\pm}$.005 & \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_signal_heatmap}\n\\caption{Quality estimation heatmap (Spearman $\\rho$) across five signals and four configurations. Left: full evaluation set; right: conditional set (non-zero F1 instances). On the full set, structural signals dominate on SciERC while all signals converge on CoNLL. On the conditional set, EM emerges as the strongest signal on both NER ($\\rho{=}0.360$) and RE ($\\rho{=}0.417$), while logprob RE collapses to near-zero ($\\rho{=}0.011$).}\n\\label{fig:signal_heatmap}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:quality_estimation} presents rank correlation ($\\rho$) and AUROC for five confidence signals across tasks and models.\n\n\\paragraph{Structural signals dominate on complex schemas.}\nOn SciERC NER, Voting achieves $\\rho{=}0.379$ and SJ $\\rho{=}0.360$, both outperforming surface ($\\rho{\\leq}0.295$) and logprob ($\\rho{=}0.205$).\nThe structural advantage persists on RE (Voting $\\rho{=}0.350$), though all signals weaken due to the 44\\% zero-F1 floor effect.\nEntity voting and SJ show moderate instance-level correlation (NER: $\\rho{=}0.659$; RE: $\\rho{=}0.605$), indicating information overlap but not redundancy; their differential behavior across difficulty levels reflects the complementary granularity of entity-level frequency vs.\\ span-level set overlap.\nWhile $\\rho{=}0.38$ explains approximately 14\\% of F1 variance, the corresponding AUROC of 0.749 indicates strong discriminative power for the binary quality/failure classification most relevant to practitioners in quality-aware routing.\\footnote{For context, semantic entropy achieves AUROC $\\approx$ 0.75--0.85 on discrete-answer QA tasks \\cite{kuhn2023semantic}; direct comparison is complicated by the fundamentally different output spaces of structured IE versus closed-form QA.}\nBootstrap CIs for signal pair differences on SciERC NER ($N{=}8$): SJ$-$logprob $\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.155$ [+0.074, +0.234]; Voting$-$SJ $\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.019$ [$-$0.033, +0.073]; SJ$-$EM $\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.065$ [+0.003, +0.130].\nThe SJ advantage over logprob is significant, while the Voting--SJ gap is not; the SJ--EM difference is marginal (CI lower bound +0.003; see Table~\\ref{tab:bootstrap_signal_ci}, Appendix~\\ref{app:bootstrap_signal} for individual signal CIs).\n\n\\paragraph{$\\rho$--AUROC dissociation.}\nOn NER, EM leads AUROC (0.749) despite ranking third in $\\rho$ (0.295); on RE, EM achieves the highest AUROC (0.706) while ranking last in $\\rho$ (0.134).\nBinary agreement provides clean separation between reliable and unreliable instances, while structural signals capture finer-grained quality variation.\nOn CoNLL with LLaMA, logprob achieves the highest AUROC (0.761) yet the lowest $\\rho$ (0.307), confirming this dissociation across datasets.\n\n\\paragraph{Signal rankings shift with task difficulty.}\nOn CoNLL (greedy F1${>}$0.91), EM leads ($\\rho{=}0.451$, AUROC${=}0.816$), surpassing SJ and Voting.\nWhen most samples are near-correct, binary agreement captures remaining variance more efficiently than set-level similarity.\nThis reversal---EM${>}$SJ on easy tasks vs.\\ SJ${>}$EM on hard tasks---demonstrates that optimal signal choice depends on task difficulty.\nCross-model stability is strong: SJ achieves $\\rho{=}0.436$ (Qwen) vs.\\ $0.431$ (LLaMA) on CoNLL ($\\Delta{=}0.005$, within 3-seed $\\sigma{\\approx}0.007$).\nOn WNUT-17, where 54\\% of instances receive zero F1, full-set correlations are misleading: voting achieves $\\rho{=}0.724$ (AUROC${=}0.913$), but this is entirely driven by the zero/non-zero boundary (conditional $\\rho{=}0.038$, n.s.; Table~\\ref{tab:wnut}).\nOn the conditional set, all signals collapse toward weak or non-significant correlations ($\\rho{\\leq}0.35$), with SJ achieving only $\\rho{=}0.089$ ($p{=}0.020$) on the full set---suggesting a capability boundary beyond which consistency signals capture binary success/failure but not quality gradation.\n\n\\paragraph{Conditional filtering.}\nFiltering to non-zero F1 instances reshuffles rankings (Figure~\\ref{fig:signal_heatmap}, right).\nEM emerges as the strongest conditional signal on both NER ($\\rho{=}0.360$) and RE ($\\rho{=}0.417$), while Voting and FK drop substantially (Voting NER: $0.379{\\to}0.300$; FK NER: $0.267{\\to}0.176$).\nLogprob RE collapses to $\\rho{=}0.011$, confirming its full-set correlation was driven by zero/non-zero separation.\nThis creates a natural complementarity: Voting and SJ are preferred on the full set, while EM provides the most precise ranking within the non-trivial subset (see Appendix~\\ref{app:additional} for details).\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\subsection{The Correlation-Selection Gap}\n\\label{sec:selection_gap}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_selection_gap}\n\\caption{The correlation-selection gap on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). Left: NER; right: RE. Bars show best-of-$N$ selection F1; the line marks quality estimation $\\rho$. Despite $\\rho$ up to 0.38, no signal reliably surpasses greedy on either task ($p{=}0.39$ for NER logprob, the best signal). Dashed line indicates oracle upper bound.}\n\\label{fig:selection_gap}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Best-of-$N$ selection F1 on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). Best non-oracle result per column in \\textbf{bold}. EM is omitted because exact-match plurality is degenerate in structured IE: with $N{=}8$, the probability of two identical structured outputs is negligible.}\n\\label{tab:selection}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & NER F1 & RE F1 \\\\\n\\midrule\nOracle & 0.776 & 0.585 \\\\\n\\midrule\nLog-prob           & \\textbf{0.650} & 0.395 \\\\\nSoft Jaccard       & 0.645 & 0.386 \\\\\nVoting             & 0.634 & 0.359 \\\\\nExact Match        & ---   & ---   \\\\\n\\midrule\nGreedy ($T{=}0$)   & 0.644 & \\textbf{0.404} \\\\\nRandom             & 0.608 & 0.354 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nDespite strong quality estimation, confidence signals yield limited gains when used for best-of-$N$ selection (Table~\\ref{tab:selection}, Figure~\\ref{fig:selection_gap}).\n\n\\paragraph{No signal reliably surpasses greedy.}\nWe exclude 22 instances with empty gold annotations, yielding $n{=}529$ for statistical analysis.\nWe evaluate statistical significance using instance-level paired bootstrap resampling ($B{=}10{,}000$, with replacement) over these instances, reporting 95\\% percentile CIs.\nThe three random seeds (42, 123, 456) refer to independent inference runs; we average $\\rho$ across seeds and report the bootstrap $p$-value of the paired difference (best signal vs.\\ greedy).\nOn NER, the oracle (0.776) reveals 13.2~pp of headroom above greedy (0.644).\nLogprob achieves the highest selection F1 (0.650, +0.6~pp), but this is not statistically significant ($p{=}0.39$; 95\\% CI: [$-$3.0, +4.2]~pp; Cohen's $d{=}0.038$; sign test: 61W/70L, win\\%${=}$46.6\\%).\nOn RE, the gap is complete: logprob achieves the highest selection F1 (0.410, +0.6~pp over greedy 0.404) but is not significant, while 18.1~pp of oracle headroom remains.\nVoting ($\\rho{=}0.379$, the best quality estimator) selects to only 0.634 on NER---\\textit{below} greedy.\nThe correlation-selection gap is consistent across all six tested configurations (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_selection}), with no signal significantly outperforming greedy in any setting.\n\n\\paragraph{Quality estimation $\\neq$ output selection.}\nInstance-level confidence signals predict \\textit{which inputs} the model handles reliably, not \\textit{which output} among $N$ candidates is best.\nWhen per-sample variance is low relative to instance-level difficulty, even perfect quality estimation cannot discriminate among near-identical candidates.\nThis gap has direct consequences for test-time scaling in IE \\cite{brown2024monkeys, snell2024scaling}: closing it likely requires per-sample signals such as trained verifiers \\cite{cobbe2021verifiers}.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\subsection{Robustness}\n\\label{sec:robustness_main}\n\nConsistency-based signals (SJ, Voting, FK) show robust performance across $T{\\in}\\{0.7, 1.0\\}$; logprob temperature sensitivity remains uncharacterized due to unavailable $T{=}0.7$ data (Table~\\ref{tab:robustness}, Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}).\nSJ NER $\\rho$ shifts by only 0.028 between $T{=}0.7$ and $T{=}1.0$ (bootstrap CI includes zero).\nDoubling from $N{=}8$ to $N{=}16$ yields sublinear gains: SJ improves by $+0.052$ on Qwen SciERC NER, while logprob is approximately $N$-invariant ($\\Delta\\rho{\\leq}0.021$).\nThe structural-over-logprob advantage is consistent across tested model families, holding across all four $N{=}16$ settings ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.135$ to ${+}0.175$).\nNER signals are reasonably well-calibrated (ECE 0.069--0.096; Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}), while RE exhibits greater miscalibration (ECE 0.109--0.339).\nFor RE at $N{=}16$, SJ, FK, and logprob all plateau at $\\rho{\\approx}0.28$, suggesting compound errors impose a ceiling.\n",

Bottom: cross-model transfer (LLaMA CoNLL $N{=}16$, 3-seed mean $\\pm\\sigma$). $^\\dagger$Mean over 3 seeds ($\\sigma{=}0.025$; individual: 0.428, 0.424, 0.384).}\n\\label{tab:robustness}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcc}\n\\toprule\n& & NER $\\rho$ & RE $\\rho$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\textit{Temperature} (Qwen SciERC, $N{=}8$)} \\\\\n\\quad SJ & $T{=}0.7$ & 0.388 & 0.234 \\\\\n\\quad SJ & $T{=}1.0$ & 0.360 & 0.250 \\\\\n\\quad FK & $T{=}0.7$ & 0.321 & 0.184 \\\\\n\\quad FK & $T{=}1.0$ & 0.267 & 0.275 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\textit{Sample size} (Qwen SciERC, $T{=}1.0$)} \\\\\n\\quad SJ & $N{=}8$  & 0.360 & 0.250 \\\\\n\\quad SJ & $N{=}16$ & 0.412$^\\dagger$ & 0.281 \\\\\n\\quad FK & $N{=}8$  & 0.267 & 0.275 \\\\\n\\quad FK & $N{=}16$ & 0.280 & 0.279 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\textit{Cross-model} (LLaMA CoNLL, $N{=}16$, 3 seeds)} \\\\\n\\quad SJ &  & $0.463{\\pm}0.007$ & --- \\\\\n\\quad FK &  & $0.455{\\pm}0.006$ & --- \\\\\n\\quad EM &  & $0.460{\\pm}0.008$ & --- \\\\\n\\quad Voting &  & $0.458{\\pm}0.003$ & --- \\\\\n\\quad Logprob &  & $0.310{\\pm}0.005$ & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_temperature_robustness}\n\\caption{Temperature robustness on SciERC ($N{=}8$). Top: NER; bottom: RE. SJ maintains stable $\\rho$ across $T{=}0.7$ and $T{=}1.0$, while FK shows mixed sensitivity. Logprob $T{=}0.7$ data unavailable.}\n\\label{fig:temperature_robustness}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Temperature sensitivity.}\nSJ NER $\\rho$ shifts from 0.388 ($T{=}0.7$) to 0.360 ($T{=}1.0$), a difference of 0.028 whose bootstrap confidence interval includes zero.\nFK shows mixed behavior: NER $\\rho$ drops by 0.054, while RE $\\rho$ increases by 0.091, likely reflecting reduced diversity at lower temperature interacting differently across tasks.\nAcross all comparisons, the SJ--FK relative ordering is preserved at both temperatures on NER (0.388 vs.\\ 0.321 at $T{=}0.7$; 0.360 vs.\\ 0.267 at $T{=}1.0$), indicating a stable signal hierarchy despite numerically small temperature effects.\n\n\\paragraph{Sample size scaling.}\nIncreasing from $N{=}8$ to $N{=}16$ improves SJ on Qwen SciERC NER: $\\rho$ rises from 0.360 to $0.412{\\pm}0.025$ (three seeds: 0.428, 0.424, 0.384), a gain of $+0.052$.\nThe magnitude of $N$-scaling gains is setting-dependent---LLaMA SciERC NER yields $+0.016$ and Qwen CoNLL NER $+0.014$---though the direction is consistently positive.\nLLaMA CoNLL exhibits the most uniform scaling: all four consistency signals gain $\\Delta\\rho{\\approx}{+}0.030$ (range: $+0.027$ to $+0.032$) while logprob gains only $+0.003$ (Table~\\ref{tab:quality_estimation}).\nFK $N$-scaling is inconsistent: degradation on SciERC ($-0.007$ to $-0.019$) but improvement on CoNLL with LLaMA ($+0.027$), suggesting sensitivity to both model and task characteristics.\nVoting confidence $N$-scaling is consistently positive ($+0.005$ on Qwen CoNLL, $+0.018$ on LLaMA SciERC, $+0.062$ on Qwen SciERC), while logprob is approximately $N$-invariant ($\\Delta{\\leq}0.021$).\nSJ, EM, and voting confidence scale consistently across all tested settings; only FK shows setting-dependent variation, suggesting that structural and binary-agreement signals are the most robust to $N$.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model transfer.}\nLLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct replicates the structural-over-surface ordering on SciERC NER (SJ $\\rho{=}0.363$, FK $\\rho{=}0.327$).\nOn CoNLL, the cross-model gap is minimal (Table~\\ref{tab:quality_estimation}): SJ achieves $\\rho{=}0.431$ (LLaMA) vs.\\ 0.436 (Qwen), $\\Delta{=}0.005$.\nAt $N{=}16$, the structural-over-logprob advantage holds across all four tested settings: Qwen SciERC (Voting $\\rho{=}0.411$ vs.\\ logprob 0.259, $\\Delta{=}{+}0.152$), Qwen CoNLL (EM $\\rho{=}0.463$ vs.\\ logprob 0.328, $\\Delta{=}{+}0.135$), LLaMA SciERC (Voting $\\rho{=}0.452$ vs.\\ logprob 0.277, $\\Delta{=}{+}0.175$), and LLaMA CoNLL 3-seed (SJ $\\rho{=}0.463{\\pm}0.007$ vs.\\ logprob $0.310{\\pm}0.005$, $\\Delta{=}{+}0.153$).\n\n\\section{Calibration Analysis}\\label{app:calibration}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_reliability}\n\\caption{Reliability diagrams on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). Top: mean F1 vs.\\ binned confidence; bottom: instance counts per bin. Left: NER; right: RE. NER signals are reasonably well-calibrated (calibration ECE 0.069--0.096), while RE shows greater miscalibration (ECE 0.109--0.339), reflecting the difficulty of calibrating confidence for compound extraction tasks.}\n\\label{fig:reliability}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nNER signals are reasonably well-calibrated (calibration ECE 0.069--0.096), while RE exhibits greater miscalibration (ECE 0.109--0.339), indicating that translating consistency scores into calibrated confidence estimates is feasible for NER but remains challenging for compound extraction tasks.\n\n\\section{Additional Analyses}\\label{app:additional}\n\n\\paragraph{Per-relation diagnostic.}\nRE signal degradation is relation-specific.\n\\textsc{Feature-Of} has near-zero correlation ($\\rho{=}0.004$), while \\textsc{Hyponym-Of} achieves $\\rho{=}0.646$.\nThe primary bottleneck is entity span errors, which account for 55.5\\% of RE false negatives---a prerequisite failure that consistency signals cannot detect, as they measure agreement among equally incorrect extractions.\nWe note that RE analysis relies on a single dataset (SciERC); generalization to other RE benchmarks remains to be validated.\n\n\\paragraph{Capability stratification.}\nFor NER, SJ effectiveness scales with structural complexity: on SciERC instances with 6+ entities, $\\rho{=}0.547$, compared to $\\rho{=}0.415$ for 1--2 entities.\nThe SJ advantage over logprob remains stable across sentence-length quartiles ($\\Delta\\rho{\\approx}0.08$--$0.20$).\nFor RE, SJ $\\rho$ increases monotonically with sentence length (Q1: 0.110 $\\to$ Q4: 0.473; Fisher $z$-test $p{=}0.004$), indicating that longer, more relation-dense sentences provide richer structure for consistency-based estimation.\nBoth proxies are weakly correlated with F1 ($|r|{<}0.15$), confirming they capture input complexity rather than directly predicting output quality; however, entity count and sentence length are themselves moderately correlated ($\\rho{=}0.650$), so they reflect overlapping rather than independent complexity dimensions.\nFor RE, entity count and relation count are strongly correlated ($\\rho{=}0.769$) and thus reflect a single structural complexity dimension rather than independent axes.\nOn the high-complexity RE subset ($n{=}81$, high relation count), signals improve substantially: Voting reaches $\\rho{=}0.630$, logprob $\\rho{=}0.554$, and SJ $\\rho{=}0.502$---all far above the full-set levels.\nRicher relational structure provides more signal for consistency-based estimation, complementing the task-level boundary established by WNUT-17: structural signals are most effective within a ``sweet spot'' of sufficient model capability and sufficient output complexity.\n\n\\paragraph{Length vs.\\ log-probability.}\nFigure~\\ref{fig:length_logprob_scatter} shows that mean log-probability is weakly positively correlated with output length (NER: $\\rho{=}0.386$, $n{=}4{,}232$; RE: $\\rho{=}0.326$, $n{=}3{,}176$), ruling out length-penalty confounding as an explanation for logprob's selection failure: longer outputs receive \\textit{higher}, not lower, token-averaged log-probability under constrained decoding.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_length_logprob_scatter}\n\\caption{Output length vs.\\ mean log-probability on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). Positive correlations (NER: $\\rho{=}0.386$; RE: $\\rho{=}0.326$) rule out length-penalty confounding.}\n\\label{fig:length_logprob_scatter}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation circularity.}\nLeave-one-out SJ (LOO-SJ), which excludes the target sample when computing confidence, yields $\\Delta\\rho{=}{-}0.014$ relative to standard SJ, with a maximum per-instance $\\Delta\\rho$ of $3.1{\\times}10^{-4}$.\nEvaluation circularity is negligible.\n\n\\paragraph{Conditional filtering details.}\nOn LLaMA conditional RE (non-zero F1 instances), SJ recovers to $\\rho{=}0.405$ while FK drops to $\\rho{=}0.259$, confirming that structural advantage is robust across both models and evaluation conditions.\nAt $N{=}16$, the divergence persists: SJ maintains $\\rho{=}0.308$ on conditional RE while logprob drops to $\\rho{=}{-}0.026$.\nEM ranks first across all four tested conditional configurations---SciERC NER ($\\rho{=}0.360$), CoNLL Qwen $N{=}16$ ($\\rho{=}0.466$), CoNLL LLaMA 3-seed ($\\rho{=}0.462$), and SciERC RE ($\\rho{=}0.417$)---despite full-set rankings ranging from \\#1 (CoNLL Qwen) to \\#5 (SciERC RE).\nOn the full set, EM is diluted by trivial agreement: instances where all $N$ samples produce identical (possibly incorrect) outputs receive EM${=}1$ regardless of quality, compressing the signal's dynamic range.\nOn the conditional set---where the model demonstrably produces varied outputs---exact agreement becomes the strongest quality signal.\nThe margin over SJ is largest on RE ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.171$), where compound extraction decisions amplify output variability, and smallest on CoNLL LLaMA ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.002$), where all signals converge near $\\rho{\\approx}0.46$.\n\n\\paragraph{WNUT-17 full signal analysis.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:wnut} reports all five signals on WNUT-17.\nVoting achieves $\\rho{=}0.724$ (AUROC${=}0.913$) on the full set, but conditional analysis (non-zero F1 instances) reveals this is entirely driven by the zero/non-zero boundary: conditional $\\rho{=}0.038$ ($p{=}0.49$).\nLogprob shows the reverse pattern: full-set $\\rho{=}{-}0.410$ but conditional $\\rho{=}0.350$, the highest among all signals on the conditional set.\nThis inversion confirms that full-set correlations on high-failure-rate datasets conflate binary success prediction with quality gradation.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Quality estimation on WNUT-17 (Qwen, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). Full: all 1{,}287 instances; Cond: non-zero F1 only.}\n\\label{tab:wnut}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nSignal & Full $\\rho$ & AUROC & Cond $\\rho$ & Cond $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nVoting  & .724 & .913 & .038 & .49 \\\\\nFK      & .125 & .555 & .169 & --- \\\\\nSJ      & .089 & .517 & .288 & --- \\\\\nEM      & .050 & .487 & .333 & --- \\\\\nLogprob & $-$.410 & .219 & .350 & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-configuration selection analysis.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_selection} extends the correlation-selection gap analysis across all six tested configurations.\nNo signal significantly outperforms greedy decoding in any setting, confirming the gap is not specific to a single model--dataset combination.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Best-of-$N$ selection F1 across configurations. $\\Delta$ = best signal $-$ greedy. No configuration yields a significant improvement. Q = Qwen, L = LLaMA, S = SciERC, C = CoNLL.}\n\\label{tab:cross_selection}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nConfig & Grdy & Best & $\\Delta$ & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQ-S NER $N{=}8$ & .644 & .650 & +.006 & .39 \\\\\nQ-S RE $N{=}8$ & .404 & .410 & +.006 & --- \\\\\nL-S NER $N{=}8$ & .646 & .652 & +.006 & .39 \\\\\nL-S NER $N{=}16$ & .645 & .649 & +.005 & n.s. \\\\\nQ-C NER $N{=}8$ & .908 & --- & $<$0 & $<$.001 \\\\\nL-C NER $N{=}8$ & .924 & --- & ${\\approx}$0 & n.s. \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Diminishing returns of $N$-scaling.}\nOur $N{=}8$ and $N{=}16$ comparisons (Table~\\ref{tab:robustness}) characterize the marginal value of additional samples for test-time scaling \\cite{brown2024monkeys, snell2024scaling}.\nFor quality estimation, structural signals benefit consistently but sublinearly: SJ $\\rho$ improves by $+0.052$ (Qwen SciERC, 3-seed mean) and $+0.032$ (LLaMA CoNLL, 3-seed mean) when doubling from $N{=}8$ to $N{=}16$, while log-probability is approximately $N$-invariant ($\\Delta\\rho{\\leq}0.021$).\nStructural signals leverage $\\binom{N}{2}$ pairwise comparisons (28 pairs at $N{=}8$, 120 at $N{=}16$), but the $\\rho$ improvement is far less than proportional, indicating diminishing information per additional comparison.\nThis asymmetry gives structural signals a relative advantage in the compute-constrained regime: at $N{=}8$, SJ already achieves $\\rho{=}0.431$ on LLaMA CoNLL (vs.\\ logprob $\\rho{=}0.307$, $\\Delta{=}{+}0.124$), and the gap \\textit{widens} at $N{=}16$ ($\\Delta{=}{+}0.153$) because structural signals continue to benefit from additional pairwise evidence while logprob does not.\nFor sample selection, additional samples increase the oracle upper bound---more draws raise the probability of a high-quality candidate---but signal-based selection remains flat because the selector's inability to identify the best candidate is a granularity limitation, not a sample size limitation.\nThe practical implication is that $N{=}8$ captures most of the achievable quality estimation benefit; further scaling widens rather than closes the correlation-selection gap absent stronger per-sample selectors.\n",

No signal reliably surpasses greedy in any configuration; CoNLL shows especially tight clustering where greedy already exceeds 0.90.}\n\\label{tab:cross_selection}\n\\begin{tabular}{llccccccc}\n\\toprule\nConfig & $n$ & Greedy & Oracle & SJ & FK & EM & LP & VC \\\\\n\\midrule\nLLaMA/SciERC $N{=}8$ & 529 & .646 & .778 & .643 & .639 & .634 & \\textbf{.652} & .633 \\\\\nLLaMA/SciERC $N{=}16$ & 529 & .645 & .806 & \\textbf{.649} & \\textbf{.649} & .640 & .646 & .640 \\\\\nQwen/CoNLL $N{=}8$ & 2{,}756 & .908 & .948 & \\textbf{.899} & .893 & .898 & .897 & .895 \\\\\nLLaMA/CoNLL $N{=}8$ & 2{,}756 & .924 & .943 & .924 & \\textbf{.925} & .924 & .924 & .923 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Notation}\\label{app:notation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:notation} summarizes the notation used throughout the paper.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{cl}\n\\toprule\nSymbol & Meaning \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\mathcal{M}$ & Language model \\\\\n$x$ & Input instance \\\\\n$y_i$ & $i$-th stochastic sample \\\\\n$N$ & Number of samples \\\\\n$T$ & Sampling temperature \\\\\n$c(\\cdot)$ & Confidence signal \\\\\nSJ & Soft Jaccard similarity \\\\\nFK & Fleiss' $\\kappa$ \\\\\nEM & Exact-match voting rate \\\\\n$c_{\\text{vote}}$ & Entity voting confidence \\\\\n$c_{\\text{lp}}$ & Log-probability (mean token log-likelihood) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Notation used throughout the paper.}\n\\label{tab:notation}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Robustness Analysis}\\label{app:robustness}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Signal robustness across configurations (Spearman $\\rho$). Top: temperature on Qwen SciERC. Middle: sample size on Qwen SciERC. Bottom: cross-model transfer (LLaMA CoNLL $N{=}16$, 3-seed mean $\\pm\\sigma$). $^\\dagger$Mean over 3 seeds ($\\sigma{=}0.025$; individual: 0.428, 0.424, 0.384).}\n\\label{tab:robustness}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcc}\n\\toprule\n& & NER $\\rho$ & RE $\\rho$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\textit{Temperature} (Qwen SciERC, $N{=}8$)} \\\\\n\\quad SJ & $T{=}0.7$ & 0.388 & 0.234 \\\\\n\\quad SJ & $T{=}1.0$ & 0.360 & 0.250 \\\\\n\\quad FK & $T{=}0.7$ & 0.321 & 0.184 \\\\\n\\quad FK & $T{=}1.0$ & 0.267 & 0.275 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\textit{Sample size} (Qwen SciERC, $T{=}1.0$)} \\\\\n\\quad SJ & $N{=}8$  & 0.360 & 0.250 \\\\\n\\quad SJ & $N{=}16$ & 0.412$^\\dagger$ & 0.281 \\\\\n\\quad FK & $N{=}8$  & 0.267 & 0.275 \\\\\n\\quad FK & $N{=}16$ & 0.280 & 0.279 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\textit{Cross-model} (LLaMA CoNLL, $N{=}16$, 3 seeds)} \\\\\n\\quad SJ &  & $0.463{\\pm}0.007$ & --- \\\\\n\\quad FK &  & $0.455{\\pm}0.006$ & --- \\\\\n\\quad EM &  & $0.460{\\pm}0.008$ & --- \\\\\n\\quad Voting &  & $0.458{\\pm}0.003$ & --- \\\\\n\\quad Logprob &  & $0.310{\\pm}0.005$ & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_temperature_robustness}\n\\caption{Temperature robustness on SciERC ($N{=}8$). Top: NER; bottom: RE. SJ maintains stable $\\rho$ across $T{=}0.7$ and $T{=}1.0$, while FK shows mixed sensitivity. Logprob $T{=}0.7$ data unavailable.}\n\\label{fig:temperature_robustness}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Temperature sensitivity.}\nSJ NER $\\rho$ shifts from 0.388 ($T{=}0.7$) to 0.360 ($T{=}1.0$), a difference of 0.028 whose bootstrap confidence interval includes zero.\nFK shows mixed behavior: NER $\\rho$ drops by 0.054, while RE $\\rho$ increases by 0.091, likely reflecting reduced diversity at lower temperature interacting differently across tasks.\nAcross all comparisons, the SJ--FK relative ordering is preserved at both temperatures on NER (0.388 vs.\\ 0.321 at $T{=}0.7$; 0.360 vs.\\ 0.267 at $T{=}1.0$), indicating a stable signal hierarchy despite numerically small temperature effects.\n\n\\paragraph{Sample size scaling.}\nIncreasing from $N{=}8$ to $N{=}16$ improves SJ on Qwen SciERC NER: $\\rho$ rises from 0.360 to $0.412{\\pm}0.025$ (three seeds: 0.428, 0.424, 0.384), a gain of $+0.052$.\nThe magnitude of $N$-scaling gains is setting-dependent---LLaMA SciERC NER yields $+0.016$ and Qwen CoNLL NER $+0.014$---though the direction is consistently positive.\nLLaMA CoNLL exhibits the most uniform scaling: all four consistency signals gain $\\Delta\\rho{\\approx}{+}0.030$ (range: $+0.027$ to $+0.032$) while logprob gains only $+0.003$ (Table~\\ref{tab:quality_estimation}).\nFK $N$-scaling is inconsistent: degradation on SciERC ($-0.007$ to $-0.019$) but improvement on CoNLL with LLaMA ($+0.027$), suggesting sensitivity to both model and task characteristics.\nVoting confidence $N$-scaling is consistently positive ($+0.005$ on Qwen CoNLL, $+0.018$ on LLaMA SciERC, $+0.062$ on Qwen SciERC), while logprob is approximately $N$-invariant ($\\Delta{\\leq}0.021$).\nSJ, EM, and voting confidence scale consistently across all tested settings; only FK shows setting-dependent variation, suggesting that structural and binary-agreement signals are the most robust to $N$.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model transfer.}\nLLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct replicates the structural-over-surface ordering on SciERC NER (SJ $\\rho{=}0.363$, FK $\\rho{=}0.327$).\nOn CoNLL, the cross-model gap is minimal (Table~\\ref{tab:quality_estimation}): SJ achieves $\\rho{=}0.431$ (LLaMA) vs.\\ 0.436 (Qwen), $\\Delta{=}0.005$.\nAt $N{=}16$, the structural-over-logprob advantage holds across all four tested settings: Qwen SciERC (Voting $\\rho{=}0.411$ vs.\\ logprob 0.259, $\\Delta{=}{+}0.152$), Qwen CoNLL (EM $\\rho{=}0.463$ vs.\\ logprob 0.328, $\\Delta{=}{+}0.135$), LLaMA SciERC (Voting $\\rho{=}0.452$ vs.\\ logprob 0.277, $\\Delta{=}{+}0.175$), and LLaMA CoNLL 3-seed (SJ $\\rho{=}0.463{\\pm}0.007$ vs.\\ logprob $0.310{\\pm}0.005$, $\\Delta{=}{+}0.153$).\n\n\\section{Calibration Analysis}\\label{app:calibration}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_reliability}\n\\caption{Reliability diagrams on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). Top: mean F1 vs.\\ binned confidence; bottom: instance counts per bin. Left: NER; right: RE. NER signals are reasonably well-calibrated (calibration ECE 0.069--0.096), while RE shows greater miscalibration (ECE 0.109--0.339), reflecting the difficulty of calibrating confidence for compound extraction tasks.}\n\\label{fig:reliability}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nNER signals are reasonably well-calibrated (calibration ECE 0.069--0.096), while RE exhibits greater miscalibration (ECE 0.109--0.339), indicating that translating consistency scores into calibrated confidence estimates is feasible for NER but remains challenging for compound extraction tasks.\n\n\\section{Additional Analyses}\\label{app:additional}\n\n\\paragraph{Per-relation diagnostic.}\nRE signal degradation is relation-specific.\n\\textsc{Feature-Of} has near-zero correlation ($\\rho{=}0.004$), while \\textsc{Hyponym-Of} achieves $\\rho{=}0.646$.\nThe primary bottleneck is entity span errors, which account for 55.5\\% of RE false negatives---a prerequisite failure that consistency signals cannot detect, as they measure agreement among equally incorrect extractions.\nWe note that RE analysis relies on a single dataset (SciERC); generalization to other RE benchmarks remains to be validated.\n\n\\paragraph{Capability stratification.}\nFor NER, SJ effectiveness scales with structural complexity: on SciERC instances with 6+ entities, $\\rho{=}0.547$, compared to $\\rho{=}0.415$ for 1--2 entities.\nThe SJ advantage over logprob remains stable across sentence-length quartiles ($\\Delta\\rho{\\approx}0.08$--$0.20$).\nFor RE, SJ $\\rho$ increases monotonically with sentence length (Q1: 0.110 $\\to$ Q4: 0.473; Fisher $z$-test $p{=}0.004$), indicating that longer, more relation-dense sentences provide richer structure for consistency-based estimation.\nBoth proxies are weakly correlated with F1 ($|r|{<}0.15$), confirming they capture input complexity rather than directly predicting output quality; however, entity count and sentence length are themselves moderately correlated ($\\rho{=}0.650$), so they reflect overlapping rather than independent complexity dimensions.\nFor RE, entity count and relation count are strongly correlated ($\\rho{=}0.769$) and thus reflect a single structural complexity dimension rather than independent axes.\nOn the high-complexity RE subset ($n{=}81$, high relation count), signals improve substantially: Voting reaches $\\rho{=}0.630$, logprob $\\rho{=}0.554$, and SJ $\\rho{=}0.502$---all far above the full-set levels.\nRicher relational structure provides more signal for consistency-based estimation, complementing the task-level boundary established by WNUT-17: structural signals are most effective within a ``sweet spot'' of sufficient model capability and sufficient output complexity.\n\n\\paragraph{Length vs.\\ log-probability.}\nFigure~\\ref{fig:length_logprob_scatter} shows that mean log-probability is weakly positively correlated with output length (NER: $\\rho{=}0.386$, $n{=}4{,}232$; RE: $\\rho{=}0.326$, $n{=}3{,}176$), ruling out length-penalty confounding as an explanation for logprob's selection failure: longer outputs receive \\textit{higher}, not lower, token-averaged log-probability under constrained decoding.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_length_logprob_scatter}\n\\caption{Output length vs.\\ mean log-probability on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). Positive correlations (NER: $\\rho{=}0.386$; RE: $\\rho{=}0.326$) rule out length-penalty confounding.}\n\\label{fig:length_logprob_scatter}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation circularity.}\nLeave-one-out SJ (LOO-SJ), which excludes the target sample when computing confidence, yields $\\Delta\\rho{=}{-}0.014$ relative to standard SJ, with a maximum per-instance $\\Delta\\rho$ of $3.1{\\times}10^{-4}$.\nEvaluation circularity is negligible.\n\n\\paragraph{Conditional filtering details.}\nOn LLaMA conditional RE (non-zero F1 instances), SJ recovers to $\\rho{=}0.405$ while FK drops to $\\rho{=}0.259$, confirming that structural advantage is robust across both models and evaluation conditions.\nAt $N{=}16$, the divergence persists: SJ maintains $\\rho{=}0.308$ on conditional RE while logprob drops to $\\rho{=}{-}0.026$.\nEM ranks first across all four tested conditional configurations---SciERC NER ($\\rho{=}0.360$), CoNLL Qwen $N{=}16$ ($\\rho{=}0.466$), CoNLL LLaMA 3-seed ($\\rho{=}0.462$), and SciERC RE ($\\rho{=}0.417$)---despite full-set rankings ranging from \\#1 (CoNLL Qwen) to \\#5 (SciERC RE).\nOn the full set, EM is diluted by trivial agreement: instances where all $N$ samples produce identical (possibly incorrect) outputs receive EM${=}1$ regardless of quality, compressing the signal's dynamic range.\nOn the conditional set---where the model demonstrably produces varied outputs---exact agreement becomes the strongest quality signal.\nThe margin over SJ is largest on RE ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.171$), where compound extraction decisions amplify output variability, and smallest on CoNLL LLaMA ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.002$), where all signals converge near $\\rho{\\approx}0.46$.\n\n\\paragraph{WNUT-17 full signal analysis.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:wnut} reports all five signals on WNUT-17.\nVoting achieves $\\rho{=}0.724$ (AUROC${=}0.913$) on the full set, but conditional analysis (non-zero F1 instances) reveals this is entirely driven by the zero/non-zero boundary: conditional $\\rho{=}0.038$ ($p{=}0.49$).\nLogprob shows the reverse pattern: full-set $\\rho{=}{-}0.410$ but conditional $\\rho{=}0.350$, the highest among all signals on the conditional set.\nThis inversion confirms that full-set correlations on high-failure-rate datasets conflate binary success prediction with quality gradation.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Quality estimation on WNUT-17 (Qwen, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$, $n{=}689$ after filtering gold-empty instances). Cond: non-zero F1 instances ($n{=}334$). Logprob exhibits strong negative full-set $\\rho$; its conditional $\\rho$ reverses to the highest among all signals, confirming that full-set correlations on high-failure-rate datasets conflate binary success prediction with quality gradation.}\n\\label{tab:wnut}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nSignal & $\\rho$ & $p$ & AUROC & Cond.\\ $\\rho$ & Cond.\\ $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nVoting  & .724 & ${<}$1e-112 & .913 & .038 & .487 \\\\\nFK      & .125 & .001 & .555 & .169 & .002 \\\\\nSJ      & .089 & .020 & .517 & .288 & ${<}$1e-7 \\\\\nEM      & .050 & .194 & .487 & .333 & ${<}$1e-9 \\\\\nLogprob & $-$.410 & ${<}$1e-28 & .219 & .350 & ${<}$1e-10 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-configuration selection analysis.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_selection} extends the correlation-selection gap analysis across all six tested configurations.\nNo signal significantly outperforms greedy decoding in any setting, confirming the gap is not specific to a single model--dataset combination.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Best-of-$N$ selection F1 across configurations. $\\Delta$ = best signal $-$ greedy. No configuration yields a significant improvement. Q = Qwen, L = LLaMA, S = SciERC, C = CoNLL.}\n\\label{tab:cross_selection}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nConfig & Grdy & Best & $\\Delta$ & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQ-S NER $N{=}8$ & .644 & .650 & +.006 & .39 \\\\\nQ-S RE $N{=}8$ & .404 & .410 & +.006 & --- \\\\\nL-S NER $N{=}8$ & .646 & .652 & +.006 & .39 \\\\\nL-S NER $N{=}16$ & .645 & .649 & +.005 & n.s. \\\\\nQ-C NER $N{=}8$ & .908 & --- & $<$0 & $<$.001 \\\\\nL-C NER $N{=}8$ & .924 & --- & ${\\approx}$0 & n.s. \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Diminishing returns of $N$-scaling.}\nOur $N{=}8$ and $N{=}16$ comparisons (Table~\\ref{tab:robustness}) characterize the marginal value of additional samples for test-time scaling \\cite{brown2024monkeys, snell2024scaling}.\nFor quality estimation, structural signals benefit consistently but sublinearly: SJ $\\rho$ improves by $+0.052$ (Qwen SciERC, 3-seed mean) and $+0.032$ (LLaMA CoNLL, 3-seed mean) when doubling from $N{=}8$ to $N{=}16$, while log-probability is approximately $N$-invariant ($\\Delta\\rho{\\leq}0.021$).\nStructural signals leverage $\\binom{N}{2}$ pairwise comparisons (28 pairs at $N{=}8$, 120 at $N{=}16$), but the $\\rho$ improvement is far less than proportional, indicating diminishing information per additional comparison.\nThis asymmetry gives structural signals a relative advantage in the compute-constrained regime: at $N{=}8$, SJ already achieves $\\rho{=}0.431$ on LLaMA CoNLL (vs.\\ logprob $\\rho{=}0.307$, $\\Delta{=}{+}0.124$), and the gap \\textit{widens} at $N{=}16$ ($\\Delta{=}{+}0.153$) because structural signals continue to benefit from additional pairwise evidence while logprob does not.\nFor sample selection, additional samples increase the oracle upper bound---more draws raise the probability of a high-quality candidate---but signal-based selection remains flat because the selector's inability to identify the best candidate is a granularity limitation, not a sample size limitation.\nThe practical implication is that $N{=}8$ captures most of the achievable quality estimation benefit; further scaling widens rather than closes the correlation-selection gap absent stronger per-sample selectors.\n",

      "content": "For quality estimation, structural signals benefit consistently but sublinearly: SJ $\\rho$ improves by $+0.052$ (Qwen SciERC, 3-seed mean) and $+0.032$ (LLaMA CoNLL, 3-seed mean) when doubling from $N{=}8$ to $N{=}16$, while log-probability is approximately $N$-invariant ($\\Delta\\rho{\\leq}0.021$).\nStructural signals leverage $\\binom{N}{2}$ pairwise comparisons (28 pairs at $N{=}8$, 120 at $N{=}16$), but the $\\rho$ improvement is far less than proportional, indicating diminishing information per additional comparison.\nThis asymmetry gives structural signals a relative advantage in the compute-constrained regime: at $N{=}8$, SJ already achieves $\\rho{=}0.431$ on LLaMA CoNLL (vs.\\ logprob $\\rho{=}0.307$, $\\Delta{=}{+}0.124$), and the gap \\textit{widens} at $N{=}16$ ($\\Delta{=}{+}0.153$) because structural signals continue to benefit from additional pairwise evidence while logprob does not.\nFor sample selection, additional samples increase the oracle upper bound---more draws raise the probability of a high-quality candidate---but signal-based selection remains flat because the selector's inability to identify the best candidate is a granularity limitation, not a sample size limitation.\nThe practical implication is that $N{=}8$ captures most of the achievable quality estimation benefit; further scaling widens rather than closes the correlation-selection gap absent stronger per-sample selectors.\n",
      "numLines": 6,
      "startLine": 185,
      "totalLines": 190
    }
  }
}

Logprob $T{=}0.7$ data unavailable.}\n\\label{fig:temperature_robustness}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Temperature sensitivity.}\nSJ NER $\\rho$ shifts from 0.388 ($T{=}0.7$) to 0.360 ($T{=}1.0$), a difference of 0.028 whose bootstrap confidence interval includes zero.\nFK shows mixed behavior: NER $\\rho$ drops by 0.054, while RE $\\rho$ increases by 0.091, likely reflecting reduced diversity at lower temperature interacting differently across tasks.\nAcross all comparisons, the SJ--FK relative ordering is preserved at both temperatures on NER (0.388 vs.\\ 0.321 at $T{=}0.7$; 0.360 vs.\\ 0.267 at $T{=}1.0$), indicating a stable signal hierarchy despite numerically small temperature effects.\n\n\\paragraph{Sample size scaling.}\nIncreasing from $N{=}8$ to $N{=}16$ improves SJ on Qwen SciERC NER: $\\rho$ rises from 0.360 to $0.412{\\pm}0.025$ (three seeds: 0.428, 0.424, 0.384), a gain of $+0.052$.\nThe magnitude of $N$-scaling gains is setting-dependent---LLaMA SciERC NER yields $+0.016$ and Qwen CoNLL NER $+0.014$---though the direction is consistently positive.\nLLaMA CoNLL exhibits the most uniform scaling: all four consistency signals gain $\\Delta\\rho{\\approx}{+}0.030$ (range: $+0.027$ to $+0.032$) while logprob gains only $+0.003$ (Table~\\ref{tab:quality_estimation}).\nFK $N$-scaling is inconsistent: degradation on SciERC ($-0.007$ to $-0.019$) but improvement on CoNLL with LLaMA ($+0.027$), suggesting sensitivity to both model and task characteristics.\nVoting confidence $N$-scaling is consistently positive ($+0.005$ on Qwen CoNLL, $+0.018$ on LLaMA SciERC, $+0.062$ on Qwen SciERC), while logprob is approximately $N$-invariant ($\\Delta{\\leq}0.021$).\nSJ, EM, and voting confidence scale consistently across all tested settings; only FK shows setting-dependent variation, suggesting that structural and binary-agreement signals are the most robust to $N$.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model transfer.}\nLLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct replicates the structural-over-surface ordering on SciERC NER (SJ $\\rho{=}0.363$, FK $\\rho{=}0.327$).\nOn CoNLL, the cross-model gap is minimal (Table~\\ref{tab:quality_estimation}): SJ achieves $\\rho{=}0.431$ (LLaMA) vs.\\ 0.436 (Qwen), $\\Delta{=}0.005$.\nAt $N{=}16$, the structural-over-logprob advantage holds across all four tested settings: Qwen SciERC (Voting $\\rho{=}0.411$ vs.\\ logprob 0.259, $\\Delta{=}{+}0.152$), Qwen CoNLL (EM $\\rho{=}0.463$ vs.\\ logprob 0.328, $\\Delta{=}{+}0.135$), LLaMA SciERC (Voting $\\rho{=}0.452$ vs.\\ logprob 0.277, $\\Delta{=}{+}0.175$), and LLaMA CoNLL 3-seed (SJ $\\rho{=}0.463{\\pm}0.007$ vs.\\ logprob $0.310{\\pm}0.005$, $\\Delta{=}{+}0.153$).\n\n\\section{Calibration Analysis}\\label{app:calibration}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_reliability}\n\\caption{Reliability diagrams on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). Top: mean F1 vs.\\ binned confidence; bottom: instance counts per bin. Left: NER; right: RE. NER signals are reasonably well-calibrated (calibration ECE 0.069--0.096), while RE shows greater miscalibration (ECE 0.109--0.339), reflecting the difficulty of calibrating confidence for compound extraction tasks.}\n\\label{fig:reliability}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nNER signals are reasonably well-calibrated (calibration ECE 0.069--0.096), while RE exhibits greater miscalibration (ECE 0.109--0.339), indicating that translating consistency scores into calibrated confidence estimates is feasible for NER but remains challenging for compound extraction tasks.\n\n\\section{Additional Analyses}\\label{app:additional}\n\n\\paragraph{Per-relation diagnostic.}\nRE signal degradation is relation-specific.\n\\textsc{Feature-Of} has near-zero correlation ($\\rho{=}0.004$), while \\textsc{Hyponym-Of} achieves $\\rho{=}0.646$.\nThe primary bottleneck is entity span errors, which account for 55.5\\% of RE false negatives---a prerequisite failure that consistency signals cannot detect, as they measure agreement among equally incorrect extractions.\nWe note that RE analysis relies on a single dataset (SciERC); generalization to other RE benchmarks remains to be validated.\n\n\\paragraph{Capability stratification.}\nFor NER, SJ effectiveness scales with structural complexity: on SciERC instances with 6+ entities, $\\rho{=}0.547$, compared to $\\rho{=}0.415$ for 1--2 entities.\nThe SJ advantage over logprob remains stable across sentence-length quartiles ($\\Delta\\rho{\\approx}0.08$--$0.20$).\nFor RE, SJ $\\rho$ increases monotonically with sentence length (Q1: 0.110 $\\to$ Q4: 0.473; Fisher $z$-test $p{=}0.004$), indicating that longer, more relation-dense sentences provide richer structure for consistency-based estimation.\nBoth proxies are weakly correlated with F1 ($|r|{<}0.15$), confirming they capture input complexity rather than directly predicting output quality; however, entity count and sentence length are themselves moderately correlated ($\\rho{=}0.650$), so they reflect overlapping rather than independent complexity dimensions.\nFor RE, entity count and relation count are strongly correlated ($\\rho{=}0.769$) and thus reflect a single structural complexity dimension rather than independent axes.\nOn the high-complexity RE subset ($n{=}81$, high relation count), signals improve substantially: Voting reaches $\\rho{=}0.630$, logprob $\\rho{=}0.554$, and SJ $\\rho{=}0.502$---all far above the full-set levels.\nRicher relational structure provides more signal for consistency-based estimation, complementing the task-level boundary established by WNUT-17: structural signals are most effective within a ``sweet spot'' of sufficient model capability and sufficient output complexity.\n\n\\paragraph{Length vs.\\ log-probability.}\nFigure~\\ref{fig:length_logprob_scatter} shows that mean log-probability is weakly positively correlated with output length (NER: $\\rho{=}0.386$, $n{=}4{,}232$; RE: $\\rho{=}0.326$, $n{=}3{,}176$), ruling out length-penalty confounding as an explanation for logprob's selection failure: longer outputs receive \\textit{higher}, not lower, token-averaged log-probability under constrained decoding.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_length_logprob_scatter}\n\\caption{Output length vs.\\ mean log-probability on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). Positive correlations (NER: $\\rho{=}0.386$; RE: $\\rho{=}0.326$) rule out length-penalty confounding.}\n\\label{fig:length_logprob_scatter}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation circularity.}\nLeave-one-out SJ (LOO-SJ), which excludes the target sample when computing confidence, yields $\\Delta\\rho{=}{-}0.014$ relative to standard SJ, with a maximum per-instance $\\Delta\\rho$ of $3.1{\\times}10^{-4}$.\nEvaluation circularity is negligible.\n\n\\paragraph{Conditional filtering details.}\nOn LLaMA conditional RE (non-zero F1 instances), SJ recovers to $\\rho{=}0.405$ while FK drops to $\\rho{=}0.259$, confirming that structural advantage is robust across both models and evaluation conditions.\nAt $N{=}16$, the divergence persists: SJ maintains $\\rho{=}0.308$ on conditional RE while logprob drops to $\\rho{=}{-}0.026$.\nEM ranks first across all four tested conditional configurations---SciERC NER ($\\rho{=}0.360$), CoNLL Qwen $N{=}16$ ($\\rho{=}0.466$), CoNLL LLaMA 3-seed ($\\rho{=}0.462$), and SciERC RE ($\\rho{=}0.417$)---despite full-set rankings ranging from \\#1 (CoNLL Qwen) to \\#5 (SciERC RE).\nOn the full set, EM is diluted by trivial agreement: instances where all $N$ samples produce identical (possibly incorrect) outputs receive EM${=}1$ regardless of quality, compressing the signal's dynamic range.\nOn the conditional set---where the model demonstrably produces varied outputs---exact agreement becomes the strongest quality signal.\nThe margin over SJ is largest on RE ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.171$), where compound extraction decisions amplify output variability, and smallest on CoNLL LLaMA ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.002$), where all signals converge near $\\rho{\\approx}0.46$.\n\n\\paragraph{WNUT-17 full signal analysis.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:wnut} reports all five signals on WNUT-17.\nVoting achieves $\\rho{=}0.724$ (AUROC${=}0.913$) on the full set, but conditional analysis (non-zero F1 instances) reveals this is entirely driven by the zero/non-zero boundary: conditional $\\rho{=}0.038$ ($p{=}0.49$).\nLogprob shows the reverse pattern: full-set $\\rho{=}{-}0.410$ but conditional $\\rho{=}0.350$, the highest among all signals on the conditional set.\nThis inversion confirms that full-set correlations on high-failure-rate datasets conflate binary success prediction with quality gradation.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Quality estimation on WNUT-17 (Qwen, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$, $n{=}689$ after filtering gold-empty instances). Cond: non-zero F1 instances ($n{=}334$). Logprob exhibits strong negative full-set $\\rho$; its conditional $\\rho$ reverses to the highest among all signals, confirming that full-set correlations on high-failure-rate datasets conflate binary success prediction with quality gradation.}\n\\label{tab:wnut}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nSignal & $\\rho$ & $p$ & AUROC & Cond.\\ $\\rho$ & Cond.\\ $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nVoting  & .724 & ${<}$1e-112 & .913 & .038 & .487 \\\\\nFK      & .125 & .001 & .555 & .169 & .002 \\\\\nSJ      & .089 & .020 & .517 & .288 & ${<}$1e-7 \\\\\nEM      & .050 & .194 & .487 & .333 & ${<}$1e-9 \\\\\nLogprob & $-$.410 & ${<}$1e-28 & .219 & .350 & ${<}$1e-10 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-configuration selection analysis.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_selection} extends the correlation-selection gap analysis with per-signal best-of-$N$ selection F1 across four additional configurations (supplementing Table~\\ref{tab:selection} for Qwen SciERC).\nNo signal reliably surpasses greedy in any setting.\nOn CoNLL, where greedy already exceeds 0.90 F1, all signals perform at or below greedy, confirming the gap is not specific to a single model--dataset combination.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Best-of-$N$ selection F1 across configurations ($T{=}1.0$). Best non-oracle signal per row in \\textbf{bold}. No signal reliably surpasses greedy in any configuration; CoNLL shows especially tight clustering where greedy already exceeds 0.90.}\n\\label{tab:cross_selection}\n\\begin{tabular}{llccccccc}\n\\toprule\nConfig & $n$ & Greedy & Oracle & SJ & FK & EM & LP & VC \\\\\n\\midrule\nLLaMA/SciERC $N{=}8$ & 529 & .646 & .778 & .643 & .639 & .634 & \\textbf{.652} & .633 \\\\\nLLaMA/SciERC $N{=}16$ & 529 & .645 & .806 & \\textbf{.649} & \\textbf{.649} & .640 & .646 & .640 \\\\\nQwen/CoNLL $N{=}8$ & 2{,}756 & .908 & .948 & ```text
60	\paragraph{Structural signals dominate on complex schemas.}
61	On SciERC NER, Voting achieves $\rho{=}0.379$ and SJ $\rho{=}0.360$, both outperforming surface ($\rho{\leq}0.295$) and logprob ($\rho{=}0.205$).
62	The structural advantage persists on RE (Voting $\rho{=}0.350$), though all signals weaken due to the 44\% zero-F1 floor effect.
63	Entity voting and SJ show moderate instance-level correlation (NER: $\rho{=}0.659$; RE: $\rho{=}0.605$), indicating information overlap but not redundancy; their differential behavior across difficulty levels reflects the complementary granularity of entity-level frequency vs.\ span-level set overlap.
64	While $\rho{=}0.38$ explains approximately 14\% of F1 variance, the corresponding AUROC of 0.749 indicates strong discriminative power for the binary quality/failure classification most relevant to practitioners in quality-aware routing.\footnote{For context, semantic entropy achieves AUROC $\approx$ 0.75--0.85 on discrete-answer QA tasks \cite{kuhn2023semantic}; direct comparison is complicated by the fundamentally different output spaces of structured IE versus closed-form QA.}
65	Bootstrap CIs for signal pair differences on SciERC NER ($N{=}8$): SJ$-$logprob $\Delta\rho{=}{+}0.155$ [+0.074, +0.234]; Voting$-$SJ $\Delta\rho{=}{+}0.019$ [$-$0.033, +0.073]; SJ$-$EM $\Delta\rho{=}{+}0.065$ [+0.003, +0.130].
66	The SJ advantage over logprob is significant, while the Voting--SJ gap is not; the SJ--EM difference is marginal (CI lower bound +0.003; see Table~\ref{tab:bootstrap_signal_ci}, Appendix~\ref{app:bootstrap_signal} for individual signal CIs).
67	
68	\paragraph{$\rho$--AUROC dissociation.}
69	On NER, EM leads AUROC (0.749) despite ranking third in $\rho$ (0.295); on RE, EM achieves the highest AUROC (0.706) while ranking last in $\rho$ (0.134).
70	Binary agreement provides clean separation between reliable and unreliable instances, while structural signals capture finer-grained quality variation.
71	On CoNLL with LLaMA, logprob achieves the highest AUROC (0.761) yet the lowest $\rho$ (0.307), confirming this dissociation across datasets.
72	
73	\paragraph{Signal rankings shift with task difficulty.}
74	On CoNLL (greedy F1${>}$0.91), EM leads ($\rho{=}0.451$, AUROC${=}0.816$), surpassing SJ and Voting.
75	When most samples are near-correct, binary agreement captures remaining variance more efficiently than set-level similarity.
76	This reversal---EM${>}$SJ on easy tasks vs.\ SJ${>}$EM on hard tasks---demonstrates that optimal signal choice depends on task difficulty.
77	Cross-model stability is strong: SJ achieves $\rho{=}0.436$ (Qwen) vs.\ $0.431$ (LLaMA) on CoNLL ($\Delta{=}0.005$, within 3-seed $\sigma{\approx}0.007$).
78	On WNUT-17, where 54\% of instances receive zero F1, full-set correlations are misleading: voting achieves $\rho{=}0.724$ (AUROC${=}0.913$), but this is entirely driven by the zero/non-zero boundary (conditional $\rho{=}0.038$, n.s.; Table~\ref{tab:wnut}).
79	Logprob exhibits strong \textit{negative} correlation ($\rho{=}{-}0.410$, AUROC${=}0.219$), indicating that higher model confidence associates with lower extraction quality on this noisy domain---further undermining logprob as a general-purpose IE quality signal.
80	On the conditional set, all signals collapse toward weak or non-significant correlations ($\rho{\leq}0.35$), with SJ achieving only $\rho{=}0.089$ ($p{=}0.020$) on the full set---suggesting a capability boundary beyond which consistency signals capture binary success/failure but not quality gradation.
81	
82	\paragraph{Conditional filtering.}
83	Filtering to non-zero F1 instances reshuffles rankings (Figure~\ref{fig:signal_heatmap}, right).
84	EM emerges as the strongest conditional signal on both NER ($\rho{=}0.360$) and RE ($\rho{=}0.417$), while Voting and FK drop substantially (Voting NER: $0.379{\to}0.300$; FK NER: $0.267{\to}0.176$).
```

```text
137	\paragraph{WNUT-17 full signal analysis.}
138	Table~\ref{tab:wnut} reports all five signals on WNUT-17.
139	Voting achieves $\rho{=}0.724$ (AUROC${=}0.913$) on the full set, but conditional analysis (non-zero F1 instances) reveals this is entirely driven by the zero/non-zero boundary: conditional $\rho{=}0.038$ ($p{=}0.49$).
140	Logprob shows the reverse pattern: full-set $\rho{=}{-}0.410$ but conditional $\rho{=}0.350$, the highest among all signals on the conditional set.
141	This inversion confirms that full-set correlations on high-failure-rate datasets conflate binary success prediction with quality gradation.
142	
143	\begin{table}[t]
144	\centering
145	\small
146	\caption{Quality estimation on WNUT-17 (Qwen, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$, $n{=}689$ after filtering gold-empty instances). Cond: non-zero F1 instances ($n{=}334$). Logprob exhibits strong negative full-set $\rho$; its conditional $\rho$ reverses to the highest among all signals, confirming that full-set correlations on high-failure-rate datasets conflate binary success prediction with quality gradation.}
147	\label{tab:wnut}
148	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
149	\toprule
150	Signal & $\rho$ & $p$ & AUROC & Cond.\ $\rho$ & Cond.\ $p$ \\
151	\midrule
152	Voting  & .724 & ${<}$1e-112 & .913 & .038 & .487 \\
153	FK      & .125 & .001 & .555 & .169 & .002 \\
154	SJ      & .089 & .020 & .517 & .288 & ${<}$1e-7 \\
155	EM      & .050 & .194 & .487 & .333 & ${<}$1e-9 \\
156	Logprob & $-$.410 & ${<}$1e-28 & .219 & .350 & ${<}$1e-10 \\
157	\bottomrule
158	\end{tabular}
159	\end{table}
160	
161	\paragraph{Cross-configuration selection analysis.}
162	Table~\ref{tab:cross_selection} extends the correlation-selection gap analysis with per-signal best-of-$N$ selection F1 across four additional configurations (supplementing Table~\ref{tab:selection} for Qwen SciERC).
163	No signal reliably surpasses greedy in any setting.
164	On CoNLL, where greedy already exceeds 0.90 F1, all signals perform at or below greedy, confirming the gap is not specific to a single model--dataset combination.
165	
166	\begin{table*}[t]
167	\centering
168	\small
169	\caption{Best-of-$N$ selection F1 across configurations ($T{=}1.0$). Best non-oracle signal per row in \textbf{bold}. No signal reliably surpasses greedy in any configuration; CoNLL shows especially tight clustering where greedy already exceeds 0.90.}
170	\label{tab:cross_selection}
171	\begin{tabular}{llccccccc}
172	\toprule
173	Config & $n$ & Greedy & Oracle & SJ & FK & EM & LP & VC \\
174	\midrule
175	LLaMA/SciERC $N{=}8$ & 529 & .646 & .778 & .643 & .639 & .634 & \textbf{.652} & .633 \\
176	LLaMA/SciERC $N{=}16$ & 529 & .645 & .806 & \textbf{.649} & \textbf{.649} & .640 & .646 & .640 \\
177	Qwen/CoNLL $N{=}8$ & 2{,}756 & .908 & .948 & \textbf{.899} & .893 & .898 & .897 & .895 \\
178	LLaMA/CoNLL $N{=}8$ & 2{,}756 & .924 & .943 & .924 & \textbf{.925} & .924 & .924 & .923 \\
179	\bottomrule
180	\end{tabular}
181	\end{table*}
182	
183	\paragraph{Diminishing returns of $N$-scaling.}
184	Our $N{=}8$ and $N{=}16$ comparisons (Table~\ref{tab:robustness}) characterize the marginal value of additional samples for test-time scaling \cite{brown2024monkeys, snell2024scaling}.
185	For quality estimation, structural signals benefit consistently but sublinearly: SJ $\rho$ improves by $+0.052$ (Qwen SciERC, 3-seed mean) and $+0.032$ (LLaMA CoNLL, 3-seed mean) when doubling from $N{=}8$ to $N{=}16$, while log-probability is approximately $N$-invariant ($\Delta\rho{\leq}0.021$).
186	Structural signals leverage $\binom{N}{2}$ pairwise comparisons (28 pairs at $N{=}8$, 120 at $N{=}16$), but the $\rho$ improvement is far less than proportional, indicating diminishing information per additional comparison.
187	This asymmetry gives structural signals a relative advantage in the compute-constrained regime: at $N{=}8$, SJ already achieves $\rho{=}0.431$ on LLaMA CoNLL (vs.\ logprob $\rho{=}0.307$, $\Delta{=}{+}0.124$), and the gap \textit{widens} at $N{=}16$ ($\Delta{=}{+}0.153$) because structural signals continue to benefit from additional pairwise evidence while logprob does not.
188	For sample selection, additional samples increase the oracle upper bound---more draws raise the probability of a high-quality candidate---but signal-based selection remains flat because the selector's inability to identify the best candidate is a granularity limitation, not a sample size limitation.
189	The practical implication is that $N{=}8$ captures most of the achievable quality estimation benefit; further scaling widens rather than closes the correlation-selection gap absent stronger per-sample selectors.
190	
191	\section{Bootstrap CI for Signal Comparisons}\label{app:bootstrap_signal}
192	
193	Table~\ref{tab:bootstrap_signal_ci} reports bootstrap confidence intervals for individual signal $\rho$ and pairwise $\Delta\rho$ on SciERC NER ($N{=}8$, $n{=}529$, $B{=}10{,}000$, seed${=}42$).
194	The SJ--logprob gap ($\Delta\rho{=}{+}0.155$) is clearly significant, confirming the structural advantage reported in \S\ref{sec:qe_results}.
195	The Voting--SJ gap ($\Delta\rho{=}{+}0.019$) is not significant, indicating that these two structural signals perform comparably.
196	The EM--SJ gap ($\Delta\rho{=}{-}0.065$) is borderline significant, with the CI lower bound at $-0.130$ and upper bound at $-0.003$.
197	
198	\begin{table}[t]
199	\centering
200	\small
201	\caption{Bootstrap 95\% CIs for signal $\rho$ on SciERC NER ($N{=}8$, $n{=}529$, $B{=}10{,}000$, seed${=}42$). Top: individual signals. Bottom: pairwise $\Delta\rho$; ``Sig.'' indicates whether the 95\% CI excludes zero.}
202	\label{tab:bootstrap_signal_ci}
203	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
204	\toprule
205	Signal & $\rho$ & 95\% CI \\
206	\midrule
207	Voting Conf. & +0.379 & [+0.299, +0.456] \\
208	Soft Jaccard & +0.360 & [+0.277, +0.437] \\
209	Exact Match & +0.295 & [+0.212, +0.371] \\
210	Fleiss' $\kappa$ & +0.267 & [+0.176, +0.354] \\
211	Log-prob & +0.205 & [+0.119, +0.291] \\
212	\bottomrule
213	\end{tabular}
214	
215	\vspace{0.5em}
216	
217	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
218	\toprule
219	Comparison & $\Delta\rho$ & 95\% CI & Sig. \\
220	\midrule
221	SJ $-$ Logprob & +0.155 & [+0.074, +0.234] & Yes \\
222	Voting $-$ SJ & +0.019 & [$-$0.033, +0.073] & No \\
223	EM $-$ SJ & $-$0.065 & [$-$0.130, $-$0.003] & Yes$^\dagger$ \\
224	\bottomrule
225	\end{tabular}

.639 & .634 & \\textbf{.652} & .633 \\\\\nLLaMA/SciERC $N{=}16$ & 529 & .645 & .806 & \\textbf{.649} & \\textbf{.649} & .640 & .646 & .640 \\\\\nQwen/CoNLL $N{=}8$ & 2{,}756 & .908 & .948 & \\textbf{.899} & .893 & .898 & .897 & .895 \\\\\nLLaMA/CoNLL $N{=}8$ & 2{,}756 & .924 & .943 & .924 & \\textbf{.925} & .924 & .924 & .923 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\paragraph{Diminishing returns of $N$-scaling.}\nOur $N{=}8$ and $N{=}16$ comparisons (Table~\\ref{tab:robustness}) characterize the marginal value of additional samples for test-time scaling \\cite{brown2024monkeys, snell2024scaling}.\nFor quality estimation, structural signals benefit consistently but sublinearly: SJ $\\rho$ improves by $+0.052$ (Qwen SciERC, 3-seed mean) and $+0.032$ (LLaMA CoNLL, 3-seed mean) when doubling from $N{=}8$ to $N{=}16$, while log-probability is approximately $N$-invariant ($\\Delta\\rho{\\leq}0.021$).\nStructural signals leverage $\\binom{N}{2}$ pairwise comparisons (28 pairs at $N{=}8$, 120 at $N{=}16$), but the $\\rho$ improvement is far less than proportional, indicating diminishing information per additional comparison.\nThis asymmetry gives structural signals a relative advantage in the compute-constrained regime: at $N{=}8$, SJ already achieves $\\rho{=}0.431$ on LLaMA CoNLL (vs.\\ logprob $\\rho{=}0.307$, $\\Delta{=}{+}0.124$), and the gap \\textit{widens} at $N{=}16$ ($\\Delta{=}{+}0.153$) because structural signals continue to benefit from additional pairwise evidence while logprob does not.\nFor sample selection, additional samples increase the oracle upper bound---more draws raise the probability of a high-quality candidate---but signal-based selection remains flat because the selector's inability to identify the best candidate is a granularity limitation, not a sample size limitation.\nThe practical implication is that $N{=}8$ captures most of the achievable quality estimation benefit; further scaling widens rather than closes the correlation-selection gap absent stronger per-sample selectors.\n\n\\section{Bootstrap CI for Signal Comparisons}\\label{app:bootstrap_signal}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:bootstrap_signal_ci} reports bootstrap confidence intervals for individual signal $\\rho$ and pairwise $\\Delta\\rho$ on SciERC NER ($N{=}8$, $n{=}529$, $B{=}10{,}000$, seed${=}42$).\nThe SJ--logprob gap ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.155$) is clearly significant, confirming the structural advantage reported in \\S\\ref{sec:qe_results}.\nThe Voting--SJ gap ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.019$) is not significant, indicating that these two structural signals perform comparably.\nThe EM--SJ gap ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{-}0.065$) is borderline significant, with the CI lower bound at $-0.130$ and upper bound at $-0.003$.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Bootstrap 95\\% CIs for signal $\\rho$ on SciERC NER ($N{=}8$, $n{=}529$, $B{=}10{,}000$, seed${=}42$). Top: individual signals. Bottom: pairwise $\\Delta\\rho$; ``Sig.'' indicates whether the 95\\% CI excludes zero.}\n\\label{tab:bootstrap_signal_ci}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nSignal & $\\rho$ & 95\\% CI \\\\\n\\midrule\nVoting Conf. & +0.379 & [+0.299, +0.456] \\\\\nSoft Jaccard & +0.360 & [+0.277, +0.437] \\\\\nExact Match & +0.295 & [+0.212, +0.371] \\\\\nFleiss' $\\kappa$ & +0.267 & [+0.176, +0.354] \\\\\nLog-prob & +0.205 & [+0.119, +0.291] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\n\\vspace{0.5em}\n\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nComparison & $\\Delta\\rho$ & 95\\% CI & Sig. \\\\\n\\midrule\nSJ $-$ Logprob & +0.155 & [+0.074, +0.234] & Yes \\\\\nVoting $-$ SJ & +0.019 & [$-$0.033, +0.073] & No \\\\\nEM $-$ SJ & $-$0.065 & [$-$0.130, $-$0.003] & Yes$^\\dagger$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n",
